I could check a guest in, swipe their card, and assign a room while basically having a full conversation with them about their flight or the weather. \n\nBut lately... man, lately it’s like my brain is just wading through molasses. I’ll be looking right at a guest's profile, and I just keep rereading the exact same screen over and over again. The words are right there, like their name and their check-out date, but the information just doesn't register in my head. And I find myself second-guessing the most incredibly simple choices. Like room notes, for example. Normally, if someone needs a feather-free room or they requested extra towels, I just type the code, hit F4, and I'm done. But now, I’ll just sit there staring at the keyboard, second-guessing simple choices like which room note to enter first. I'll actually debate with myself if I should put the late check-in note before the crib request, which literally does not matter at all, the housekeeping staff sees it all the same. \n\nBut I'll just freeze up, staring at the blinking cursor, wondering if I already typed it or if I'm putting it in the wrong field. And it's just a simple room note! It shouldn't take me three minutes to process something I've done a thousand times. But I just can't seem to hold my focus, and then I realize the guest is just standing there staring at me while I look at the screen, and I have to apologize and pretend the computer is being slow. It's just... I don't know, it's like my working memory is just completely shot right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do outside of work due to the way you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I was saying earlier about the trivia league, I'm still going to that. And I actually really enjoy it still. It's not like I'm sitting there at the pub dreading being there or anything. I still get really into it, especially the history rounds, because I watch a lot of those documentaries on streaming services—you know, the ones with the dramatic reenactments? I can binge those for hours on a Sunday afternoon. So when a history question comes up, I'm usually the one at the table who knows it, unless it's something really obscure about, like, 14th-century agricultural practices or something, but usually it's more mainstream history. \n\nAnd I'm still doing my regular stuff, like I said, the grocery store, the gym... I mean, I actually went to this outdoor farmer's market thing last weekend with a couple of friends. It was over in that park by the river, the one they just renovated with the new walking paths? They had all these local vendors selling artisanal cheeses and homemade jams and stuff. I ended up buying this really incredible spicy peach salsa that I've been putting on basically everything since Sunday. So, yeah, I was walking around there for like two hours, just enjoying the nice weather and chatting with my friends, and I didn't feel weird or disconnected or anything. \n\nIt really is just... it's just work. That's the only place where I feel this way. The second I walk out those sliding glass doors at the end of my shift, it's like this massive weight just lifts right off my shoulders, and I can breathe again. I can go home, I can watch my shows, I can go out for a burger with my buddy Dave—he always wants to go to this place that has these ridiculously huge onion rings, which I love, but they always give me terrible heartburn, but I eat them anyway. So, yeah, outside of that building, I'm still me. I'm still doing all my normal stuff and actually having fun doing it. It's just... it's just when I'm on the clock that everything feels so messed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your reactions to noises and memories of the incident are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... outside of work, no, like I was saying with the trivia and the farmer's market, my daily life is perfectly fine. But at work? Yeah. Yeah, it's definitely becoming a problem. I mean, I’m still showing up, right? I haven't called out sick or anything, because I really pride myself on being reliable. In the five years I've worked there, I've maybe called in sick twice, and one of those was when I had that awful stomach flu that was going around last winter. So I am still clocking in for my scheduled days. \n\nBut my manager—he's usually pretty observant about this stuff, he's been managing hotels for like twenty years—he actually had to shorten a couple of my shifts this past week. He pulled me aside into the back office, the one with the really squeaky chair that we keep meaning to replace, and said he noticed I was just... struggling. Because, like I mentioned with the computer system earlier, I’m just so much slower at the reservation desk right now. It's taking me forever to get through a line of check-ins, and he could see the queue backing up all the way to the revolving doors, which is a huge metric they track for guest satisfaction. \n\nAnd the other thing, and this is probably the main reason he sent me home early on Tuesday... I keep finding excuses not to cross the lobby. Which is a huge part of the job! Like, if a guest needs help with the self-serve checkout kiosk, or if someone leaves the local attraction brochure rack all messy over by the concierge stand, normally I'd just pop out from behind the desk, walk across the main floor—which is right where it all happened, right in the center of that big open space—and take care of it. But now? I'll literally pretend I have to restock the blank room keys, or I'll ask one of the bellhops to go do it, or I'll just suddenly decide I desperately need to reorganize the lost and found drawer in the back. I just... I can't bring myself to walk out into that open space where guests are milling around. I feel too exposed. So I just stay glued to my terminal, and my manager definitely noticed I wasn't circulating and helping people in the lobby like I'm supposed to. So, yeah, it's definitely interfering with my actual job duties right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with your relationships with friends or family because of how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not at all. My relationships are totally fine. Like I was saying about Dave, the guy who always wants those massive onion rings? We actually hung out just yesterday, too. He was telling me all about his new car—he just bought this slightly used sedan, a hybrid, because he's got this crazy long commute now since his company moved their main office way out into the suburbs... but anyway, we were talking at the diner for like two or three hours, and it was totally normal. I didn't feel snappy or irritable with him, and I was completely engaged in what he was saying. \n\nAnd my family is good, too. My mom calls me every Sunday evening, practically like clockwork. Usually right after she finishes watching her Sunday night British mystery shows, she'll call me up and we talk for a good forty-five minutes about everything and nothing. I didn't feel disconnected from her at all. She didn't even notice anything was off with me, because, honestly, when I'm at home on the phone with her, nothing really is off. \n\nSo, yeah, nobody's mad at me, I'm not pulling away from anyone or getting into arguments. My sister even texted me this morning to see if I wanted to come over for a backyard barbecue this weekend because her kids are finally out of school for the summer, and I immediately said yes. I'm actually really looking forward to it. I'm supposed to bring a potato salad. I usually just buy the one from the deli down the street from my apartment because I'm absolutely terrible at boiling potatoes—they always end up either way too mushy or they're still crunchy in the middle, which is just the worst texture, you know? But yeah, no, my friends and family... everything is completely fine there. It really is just that building. Just the hotel. No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't... I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or that it was my fault or anything like that. I know I'm just the front desk clerk, you know? Sure, you get the occasional grumpy guest who yells at you because the ice machine on the fourth floor is broken—which, honestly, it's been broken for like three months and maintenance keeps saying they're waiting on a part from overseas, which I'm starting to think is just an excuse because how long does it take to ship a plastic valve, right? But even when guests get snappy about stuff like that, I don't think they're fundamentally bad people. They're just tired from traveling, or their flight got delayed, or whatever. 

So, no, I don't have any dark, looming thoughts about humanity or myself or anything. I don't hate myself, I don't hate the guests, I don't think I'm broken. I'm just a normal guy trying to do my shifts and pay the rent on my apartment—which my landlord just raised by fifty bucks a month, by the way, which is super annoying, but what can you do? But yeah, no, my thoughts about myself and other people are pretty much exactly the same as they've always been.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, thank you. Um... well, to be honest, I wasn't even entirely sure if I should make an appointment, but... it's just been really getting to me, especially at work. I work as an information clerk, kind of a receptionist role, down at this really busy building downtown. And, you know, usually it's fine, I actually really like the job. I took some college classes a while back and it just sort of fit what I needed at the time, getting to talk to people and help them out. \n\nBut lately... well, what really pushed me to finally pick up the phone and come in today is... it's the noises. It's just so exhausting. Like, every single time a door slams—and we have these really heavy glass lobby doors that people just let swing shut behind them so they just bang, you know?—or, or even if someone is just in a hurry and they roll one of those hard plastic suitcases really fast across the tile in the lobby... the sound of the wheels clacking on the floor... I just, I jump. \n\nI mean, I jump so hard, like... like it’s happening all over again. And it's not just a little normal flinch where you brush it off. It's my whole body. My heart just completely takes off, pounding out of my chest, and it literally takes me a solid minute, sometimes longer, just to get my heart back down and catch my breath. And in a lobby, people are always coming and going, so I'm just sitting there all day bracing myself, and every time there's a sudden noise I'm practically leaping out of my chair... it's just really wearing me out.\n\nDoctor: When you say it's like it's happening all over again, what exactly do you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I should explain what happened. About, oh, I don't know, a couple of weeks ago, we had this really angry guest. I mean, people get upset sometimes, usually it's about parking validation or they can't find the right office suite because the directory in our building is honestly so confusing, they haven't updated it since they renovated the third floor back in 2018. But this guy... he was just on another level. He came storming up to my desk, yelling about some appointment he missed because of the elevators, which, I mean, I don't control the elevators, but you know how people are when they're stressed. \n\nAnyway, he was just screaming, and then he leaned right over the counter—like, his face was maybe a foot from mine—and he just violently shoved all the equipment on the desk. The credit card reader, the heavy acrylic sign holder, the little metal bell... he just swept his arm and knocked it all right at me. It all crashed to the floor, and some of it hit me, and the noise was just deafening in that echoey lobby. The security guard had to come over and physically escort him out, and I just sat there shaking for the rest of my shift. \n\nSo now... when I say it's happening all over again... it's like, out of nowhere, I'll just get this crystal clear picture in my head of him leaning over that counter. His face, the way his arm swung, the exact sound of that plastic sign cracking against the tile. It just flashes in my mind. And it's not even like I'm sitting there thinking about it or dwelling on it. I could literally just be trying to answer a totally normal phone call, you know, \"Good morning, downtown plaza,\" just doing my job, and boom, the image is just right there in my head, playing on a loop. And then my heart starts racing all over again, just like I was talking about with the noises. It's just so intrusive, you know? I can't seem to stop it from popping up.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned getting a crystal clear picture in your head of the angry guest, does this feel like you're reliving the moment or just remembering it?\n\nPatient: Um... well, that's an interesting way to put it. I wouldn't say I'm actually reliving it, like... I'm not transported back in time or anything like that. I know I'm still sitting at my desk in the present. Like, I can still feel the armrests on my chair—which, by the way, is this awful squeaky mesh chair that I've been trying to get building management to replace for like six months, but they always say it's not in the budget for this quarter, which is ridiculous given what they charge for parking. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know I'm in my chair. I can see my computer monitor, I usually have the visitor log open, you know, the main spreadsheet where we track the temporary contractor badges. So I know exactly where I am, and I know it's just a memory. But it's just... such a loud memory, if that makes sense? It's like watching a movie in really crisp high definition, where you know you're just sitting in your living room watching a screen, but the picture is so sharp and the sound is so sudden that it startles you anyway. \n\nLike, I'm not hallucinating the guy. I don't think he's literally standing there in front of me again. It's just that the memory is so aggressive. My brain just shoves that image of him swiping the desk right to the absolute front of my mind, and even though my logical brain knows it's just a memory of something that already happened, my body still does that whole heart-pounding, jumping thing I mentioned earlier. But yeah, no, to answer your question, I'm definitely just remembering it. It's just a really, really intense way of remembering. Because normally when you remember something, like what you did over the weekend or what you had for dinner, it's kind of fuzzy and distant, right? But this is just... boom, every single detail. The exact shade of blue on his tie, the little scratch on the corner of the acrylic sign right before it hit the floor... it's all just right there, taking over my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding going to work or certain areas of the building since the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't stopped going to work. I mean, I really need the paycheck, and like I said before, I do actually like the job most days. Plus, my supervisor has been pretty understanding about the whole thing, so I haven't called out sick or anything like that. But... as for avoiding certain areas of the building... yeah. I definitely have been doing that. \n\nThere's this back hallway in the building—it's mostly just used by the maintenance staff and the delivery guys bringing packages for the upper suites. It connects the loading dock straight through to the employee breakroom and the lockers, so it completely bypasses the main atrium. It's honestly kind of dingy, and the fluorescent lights back there have this awful flicker that used to drive me completely crazy, but lately... I've been taking that back hallway whenever I possibly can. Every morning when I arrive, when I go on my lunch break, when I leave for the day... I just take the long way around. \n\nBecause if I try to walk through the regular way, through the main lobby... especially if I have to walk past that exact spot by the front of the counter where he was standing... my chest just gets so incredibly tight. It's like this sudden, heavy pressure right in the middle of my ribs, and I just get this overwhelming feeling that I need to get away from it. I just want to turn around and bolt in the other direction. So, yeah, rather than deal with that awful tight feeling and the stress of being in that exact space where it all crashed down, I just stick to the maintenance hall. It adds like five or ten minutes to my walk from the parking garage, depending on if the loading dock doors are propped open or not, but I just can't bring myself to walk past that spot in the lobby right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares about the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Um... nightmares? No, I haven't really had any nightmares about it. I mean, my sleep hasn't been great, honestly. I keep waking up feeling kind of restless, and the other night I woke up because the neighbor's dog was barking—it's this little terrier mix, super yappy, they leave it out on the patio sometimes which I really don't think is allowed by the HOA, but whatever. Anyway, I woke up from that and just couldn't get back to sleep for like two hours. I just laid there staring at the ceiling fan, watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside. But as far as actually dreaming about the guy at work, or the desk, or the stuff crashing down? No, nothing like that. \n\nI usually remember my dreams pretty well, too. Like, just last week I had this completely bizarre dream that I was trying to organize a filing cabinet, but all the folders were made of actual slices of bread, and I was getting so stressed out because the mayonnaise was getting all over the documents. Which, I guess, is a stress dream, but it definitely wasn't about the lobby incident. So, yeah, no nightmares about the angry guest or anything. When I'm asleep, my brain seems to just do its own weird thing, it's really just when I'm awake that the images keep popping up like I was saying before.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any social activities or places outside of work since the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Um... outside of work? No, not really. I mean, my social life hasn't really changed much. I still go to my Thursday night trivia league at this pub down the street from my apartment—we actually came in second place last week, missed first by one point because we couldn't remember the capital of North Dakota, which is Bismarck, obviously, I should have known that. And I still go to the grocery store, the gym... well, I say the gym, I really only go like twice a week and mostly just walk on the treadmill while listening to podcasts. But I haven't stopped doing any of that stuff. \n\nIt's really just... like I was saying before, it's just the lobby at work. That's the only place I'm avoiding. I just keep taking that dingy back hallway with the flickering fluorescent lights that I mentioned. Because, like I said, if I even try to walk past that exact spot by the front counter where the guy was standing... where everything just went flying... my chest just gets so incredibly tight. It's like this heavy pressure, and I just get this overwhelming urge to bolt, to just get away from that specific area. So outside of work, I'm totally fine, going about my normal business, running errands, seeing friends. But inside the building? Yeah, I'm just sticking to the maintenance hallway to avoid that spot in the lobby. It's just easier that way, you know? Why put myself through that tight-chest feeling if I can just take a detour past the loading dock?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself startling more easily in general, not just in response to noises that remind you of the incident?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, honestly, that's... that's actually the main reason I decided I needed to come in and talk to someone. Because it's not just the lobby, it's... it's kind of everywhere now, and it's exhausting. Like, if a door slams—even if it's just the wind catching the heavy glass doors at the entrance, or the maintenance guy letting the utility closet door slip from his hand—I just practically jump out of my skin. And suitcases, oh man, the suitcases. We get a lot of people coming straight from the airport with those hard-shell roller bags, right? And the tile in our building has these wide grout lines, so when they roll them fast, it makes this loud, clattering *thwack-thwack-thwack* sound. \n\nBefore the incident, I wouldn't have even blinked at it. It was just background noise, just part of the ambient sound of the building. But now? Every single time someone comes rushing in with one of those bags, or if a door shuts a little too hard, or even if someone just drops a thick stack of mail on a desk nearby... I flinch so hard my shoulders end up by my ears. It's like my brain instantly thinks, *Oh god, it's happening again, he's throwing things again.* And then my heart just starts hammering against my ribs, just racing like I ran a sprint, and I have to sit there for like a full minute, taking these deep breaths, just trying to get my heart rate back down to normal. It's incredibly embarrassing if someone sees me do it, too. I just look so jumpy and on edge all the time, over completely normal, everyday sounds. It's just... I can't seem to turn off that alarm bell in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on your work or daily tasks since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It’s been so frustrating, to be honest with you. Because normally, I’m pretty sharp at the desk. I mean, I’ve been doing this for a while, so I know our reservation system inside and out—it's this older, kind of clunky software where you have to use a lot of F-keys instead of just clicking a mouse, but I used to just fly right through it. At the desk I used to move through reservations pretty smoothly, you know? I could check a guest in, swipe their card, and assign a room while basically having a full conversation with them about their flight or the weather. \n\nBut lately... man, lately it’s like my brain is just wading through molasses. I’ll be looking right at a guest's profile, and I just keep rereading the exact same screen over and over again. The words are right there, like their name and their check-out date, but the information just doesn't register in my head. And I find myself second-guessing the most incredibly simple choices. Like room notes, for example. Normally, if someone needs a feather-free room or they requested extra towels, I just type the code, hit F4, and I'm done. But now, I’ll just sit there staring at the keyboard, second-guessing simple choices like which room note to enter first. I'll actually debate with myself if I should put the late check-in note before the crib request, which literally does not matter at all, the housekeeping staff sees it all the same. \n\nBut I'll just freeze up, staring at the blinking cursor, wondering if I already typed it or if I'm putting it in the wrong field. And it's just a simple room note! It shouldn't take me three minutes to process something I've done a thousand times. But I just can't seem to hold my focus, and then I realize the guest is just standing there staring at me while I look at the screen, and I have to apologize and pretend the computer is being slow. It's just... I don't know, it's like my working memory is just completely shot right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do outside of work due to the way you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I was saying earlier about the trivia league, I'm still going to that. And I actually really enjoy it still. It's not like I'm sitting there at the pub dreading being there or anything. I still get really into it, especially the history rounds, because I watch a lot of those documentaries on streaming services—you know, the ones with the dramatic reenactments? I can binge those for hours on a Sunday afternoon. So when a history question comes up, I'm usually the one at the table who knows it, unless it's something really obscure about, like, 14th-century agricultural practices or something, but usually it's more mainstream history. \n\nAnd I'm still doing my regular stuff, like I said, the grocery store, the gym... I mean, I actually went to this outdoor farmer's market thing last weekend with a couple of friends. It was over in that park by the river, the one they just renovated with the new walking paths? They had all these local vendors selling artisanal cheeses and homemade jams and stuff. I ended up buying this really incredible spicy peach salsa that I've been putting on basically everything since Sunday. So, yeah, I was walking around there for like two hours, just enjoying the nice weather and chatting with my friends, and I didn't feel weird or disconnected or anything. \n\nIt really is just... it's just work. That's the only place where I feel this way. The second I walk out those sliding glass doors at the end of my shift, it's like this massive weight just lifts right off my shoulders, and I can breathe again. I can go home, I can watch my shows, I can go out for a burger with my buddy Dave—he always wants to go to this place that has these ridiculously huge onion rings, which I love, but they always give me terrible heartburn, but I eat them anyway. So, yeah, outside of that building, I'm still me. I'm still doing all my normal stuff and actually having fun doing it. It's just... it's just when I'm on the clock that everything feels so messed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your reactions to noises and memories of the incident are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... outside of work, no, like I was saying with the trivia and the farmer's market, my daily life is perfectly fine. But at work? Yeah. Yeah, it's definitely becoming a problem. I mean, I’m still showing up, right? I haven't called out sick or anything, because I really pride myself on being reliable. In the five years I've worked there, I've maybe called in sick twice, and one of those was when I had that awful stomach flu that was going around last winter. So I am still clocking in for my scheduled days. \n\nBut my manager—he's usually pretty observant about this stuff, he's been managing hotels for like twenty years—he actually had to shorten a couple of my shifts this past week. He pulled me aside into the back office, the one with the really squeaky chair that we keep meaning to replace, and said he noticed I was just... struggling. Because, like I mentioned with the computer system earlier, I’m just so much slower at the reservation desk right now. It's taking me forever to get through a line of check-ins, and he could see the queue backing up all the way to the revolving doors, which is a huge metric they track for guest satisfaction. \n\nAnd the other thing, and this is probably the main reason he sent me home early on Tuesday... I keep finding excuses not to cross the lobby. Which is a huge part of the job! Like, if a guest needs help with the self-serve checkout kiosk, or if someone leaves the local attraction brochure rack all messy over by the concierge stand, normally I'd just pop out from behind the desk, walk across the main floor—which is right where it all happened, right in the center of that big open space—and take care of it. But now? I'll literally pretend I have to restock the blank room keys, or I'll ask one of the bellhops to go do it, or I'll just suddenly decide I desperately need to reorganize the lost and found drawer in the back. I just... I can't bring myself to walk out into that open space where guests are milling around. I feel too exposed. So I just stay glued to my terminal, and my manager definitely noticed I wasn't circulating and helping people in the lobby like I'm supposed to. So, yeah, it's definitely interfering with my actual job duties right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with your relationships with friends or family because of how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not at all. My relationships are totally fine. Like I was saying about Dave, the guy who always wants those massive onion rings? We actually hung out just yesterday, too. He was telling me all about his new car—he just bought this slightly used sedan, a hybrid, because he's got this crazy long commute now since his company moved their main office way out into the suburbs... but anyway, we were talking at the diner for like two or three hours, and it was totally normal. I didn't feel snappy or irritable with him, and I was completely engaged in what he was saying. \n\nAnd my family is good, too. My mom calls me every Sunday evening, practically like clockwork. Usually right after she finishes watching her Sunday night British mystery shows, she'll call me up and we talk for a good forty-five minutes about everything and nothing. Like this past week, she was going on and on about her neighbor's dog digging up her petunias, and I was just sitting there on my couch, listening and laughing with her. I didn't feel disconnected from her at all. She didn't even notice anything was off with me, because, honestly, when I'm at home on the phone with her, nothing really is off. \n\nSo, yeah, nobody's mad at me, I'm not pulling away from anyone or getting into arguments. My sister even texted me this morning to see if I wanted to come over for a backyard barbecue this weekend because her kids are finally out of school for the summer, and I immediately said yes. I'm actually really looking forward to it. I'm supposed to bring a potato salad. I usually just buy the one from the deli down the street from my apartment because I'm absolutely terrible at boiling potatoes—they always end up either way too mushy or they're still crunchy in the middle, which is just the worst texture, you know? But yeah, no, my friends and family... everything is completely fine there. It really is just that building. Just the hotel. Outside of that, I'm just regular old me, hanging out and chatting with everyone just exactly like I always do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having persistent negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't... I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or that it was my fault or anything like that. I know I'm just the front desk clerk, you know? I was just doing my job. And as for other people... I mean, I don't think everyone is suddenly out to get me or that the world is this horribly evil place now. I know some people get really cynical after something crazy happens to them. Like, my uncle Gary, he got scammed by this shady roofing contractor a few years ago—the guy took his deposit and just completely vanished, disconnected his phone and everything—and ever since then, Gary thinks literally everyone is a criminal trying to pick his pocket. He won't even let the neighborhood kids shovel his driveway in the winter because he thinks it's some kind of racket. \n\nBut I'm not like that. I still think people are generally pretty decent. Sure, you get the occasional grumpy guest who yells at you because the ice machine on the fourth floor is broken—which, honestly, it's been broken for like three months and maintenance keeps saying they're waiting on a part from overseas, which I'm starting to think is just an excuse because how long does it take to ship a plastic valve, right? But even when guests get snappy about stuff like that, I don't think they're fundamentally bad people. They're just tired from traveling, or their flight got delayed, or whatever. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any dark, looming thoughts about humanity or myself or anything. I don't hate myself, I don't hate the guests, I don't think I'm broken. I'm just a normal guy trying to do my shifts and pay the rent on my apartment—which my landlord just raised by fifty bucks a month, by the way, which is super annoying, but what can you do? But yeah, no, my thoughts about myself and other people are pretty much exactly the same as they've always been. It's just my reaction to being out in that specific open lobby space that's the problem right now, not how I actually think about people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So I just stay glued to my terminal, and my manager definitely noticed I wasn't circulating and helping people in the lobby like I'm supposed to. He was telling me all about his new car—he just bought this slightly used sedan, a hybrid, because he's got this crazy long commute now since his company moved their main office way out into the suburbs... but anyway, we were talking at the diner for like two or three hours, and it was totally normal. I didn't feel snappy or irritable with him, and I was completely engaged in what he was saying. \n\nAnd my family is good, too. My mom calls me every Sunday evening, practically like clockwork. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not outside of work. Like I said, the farmer's market, the diner, my apartment... everywhere else is completely fine. It really is just the hotel. But even inside the hotel, it's not like I'm avoiding the break room or the supply closet or anything. It's really just... that specific spot. The lobby. 

Like, for instance, when I come in for my shift, I usually park in the employee lot out back, right? And normally, I'd just walk through the main entrance, say hi to the bellmen, maybe grab a coffee from the little cafe stand in the corner of the lobby before I clock in. But lately... I just can't do it. I've actually started taking the back hallway. It's this long, dingy corridor that runs behind the kitchens and the laundry room—it always smells faintly of bleach and old cooking oil, and the fluorescent lights in there flicker constantly, which gives me a bit of a headache if I look at them too long. But I've been taking that route every single day since it happened. 

Because if I try to walk through the main lobby, especially past that one spot right near the grand staircase where... you know, where the guy was screaming and throwing things... my chest just tightens up. It's like this immediate, physical reaction. My breathing gets shallow, my heart starts pounding, and I just get this overwhelming urge to turn around and get as far away from that spot as possible. It's ridiculous, I know, because it's just a floor with a rug on it, but I just can't bring myself to walk past it if I don't absolutely have to. So, yeah, the back hallway it is, even if it means dodging laundry carts and dealing with the flickering lights. It's just easier than feeling like I can't breathe out in the main lobby.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, thank you. Um... well, to be honest, I wasn't even entirely sure if I should make an appointment, but... it's just been really getting to me, especially at work. I work as an information clerk, kind of a receptionist role, down at this really busy building downtown. And, you know, usually it's fine, I actually really like the job. I took some college classes a while back and it just sort of fit what I needed at the time, getting to talk to people and help them out. \n\nBut lately... well, what really pushed me to finally pick up the phone and come in today is... it's the noises. It's just so exhausting. Like, every single time a door slams—and we have these really heavy glass lobby doors that people just let swing shut behind them so they just bang, you know?—or, or even if someone is just in a hurry and they roll one of those hard plastic suitcases really fast across the tile in the lobby... the sound of the wheels clacking on the floor... I just, I jump. \n\nI mean, I jump so hard, like... like it’s happening all over again. And it's not just a little normal flinch where you brush it off. It's my whole body. My heart just completely takes off, pounding out of my chest, and it literally takes me a solid minute, sometimes longer, just to get my heart back down and catch my breath. And in a lobby, people are always coming and going, so I'm just sitting there all day bracing myself, and every time there's a sudden noise I'm practically leaping out of my chair... it's just really wearing me out.\n\nDoctor: When you say it's like it's happening all over again, what exactly do you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I should explain what happened. About, oh, I don't know, a couple of weeks ago, we had this really angry guest. I mean, people get upset sometimes, usually it's about parking validation or they can't find the right office suite because the directory in our building is honestly so confusing, they haven't updated it since they renovated the third floor back in 2018. But this guy... he was just on another level. He came storming up to my desk, yelling about some appointment he missed because of the elevators, which, I mean, I don't control the elevators, but you know how people are when they're stressed. \n\nAnyway, he was just screaming, and then he leaned right over the counter—like, his face was maybe a foot from mine—and he just violently shoved all the equipment on the desk. The credit card reader, the heavy acrylic sign holder, the little metal bell... he just swept his arm and knocked it all right at me. It all crashed to the floor, and some of it hit me, and the noise was just deafening in that echoey lobby. The security guard had to come over and physically escort him out, and I just sat there shaking for the rest of my shift. \n\nSo now... when I say it's happening all over again... it's like, out of nowhere, I'll just get this crystal clear picture in my head of him leaning over that counter. His face, the way his arm swung, the exact sound of that plastic sign cracking against the tile. It just flashes in my mind. And it's not even like I'm sitting there thinking about it or dwelling on it. I could literally just be trying to answer a totally normal phone call, you know, \"Good morning, downtown plaza,\" just doing my job, and boom, the image is just right there in my head, playing on a loop. And then my heart starts racing all over again, just like I was talking about with the noises. It's just so intrusive, you know? I can't seem to stop it from popping up.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned getting a crystal clear picture in your head of the angry guest, does this feel like you're reliving the moment or just remembering it?\n\nPatient: Um... well, that's an interesting way to put it. I wouldn't say I'm actually reliving it, like... I'm not transported back in time or anything like that. I know I'm still sitting at my desk in the present. Like, I can still feel the armrests on my chair—which, by the way, is this awful squeaky mesh chair that I've been trying to get building management to replace for like six months, but they always say it's not in the budget for this quarter, which is ridiculous given what they charge for parking. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I know I'm in my chair. I can see my computer monitor, I usually have the visitor log open, you know, the main spreadsheet where we track the temporary contractor badges. So I know exactly where I am, and I know it's just a memory. But it's just... such a loud memory, if that makes sense? It's like watching a movie in really crisp high definition, where you know you're just sitting in your living room watching a screen, but the picture is so sharp and the sound is so sudden that it startles you anyway. \n\nLike, I'm not hallucinating the guy. I don't think he's literally standing there in front of me again. It's just that the memory is so aggressive. My brain just shoves that image of him swiping the desk right to the absolute front of my mind, and even though my logical brain knows it's just a memory of something that already happened, my body still does that whole heart-pounding, jumping thing I mentioned earlier. But yeah, no, to answer your question, I'm definitely just remembering it. It's just a really, really intense way of remembering. Because normally when you remember something, like what you did over the weekend or what you had for dinner, it's kind of fuzzy and distant, right? But this is just... boom, every single detail. The exact shade of blue on his tie, the little scratch on the corner of the acrylic sign right before it hit the floor... it's all just right there, taking over my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding going to work or certain areas of the building since the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't stopped going to work. I mean, I really need the paycheck, and like I said before, I do actually like the job most days. Plus, my supervisor has been pretty understanding about the whole thing, so I haven't called out sick or anything like that. But... as for avoiding certain areas of the building... yeah. I definitely have been doing that. \n\nThere's this back hallway in the building—it's mostly just used by the maintenance staff and the delivery guys bringing packages for the upper suites. It connects the loading dock straight through to the employee breakroom and the lockers, so it completely bypasses the main atrium. It's honestly kind of dingy, and the fluorescent lights back there have this awful flicker that used to drive me completely crazy, but lately... I've been taking that back hallway whenever I possibly can. Every morning when I arrive, when I go on my lunch break, when I leave for the day... I just take the long way around. \n\nBecause if I try to walk through the regular way, through the main lobby... especially if I have to walk past that exact spot by the front of the counter where he was standing... my chest just gets so incredibly tight. It's like this sudden, heavy pressure right in the middle of my ribs, and I just get this overwhelming feeling that I need to get away from it. I just want to turn around and bolt in the other direction. So, yeah, rather than deal with that awful tight feeling and the stress of being in that exact space where it all crashed down, I just stick to the maintenance hall. It adds like five or ten minutes to my walk from the parking garage, depending on if the loading dock doors are propped open or not, but I just can't bring myself to walk past that spot in the lobby right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares about the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Um... nightmares? No, I haven't really had any nightmares about it. I mean, my sleep hasn't been great, honestly. I keep waking up feeling kind of restless, and the other night I woke up because the neighbor's dog was barking—it's this little terrier mix, super yappy, they leave it out on the patio sometimes which I really don't think is allowed by the HOA, but whatever. Anyway, I woke up from that and just couldn't get back to sleep for like two hours. I just laid there staring at the ceiling fan, watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside. But as far as actually dreaming about the guy at work, or the desk, or the stuff crashing down? No, nothing like that. \n\nI usually remember my dreams pretty well, too. Like, just last week I had this completely bizarre dream that I was trying to organize a filing cabinet, but all the folders were made of actual slices of bread, and I was getting so stressed out because the mayonnaise was getting all over the documents. Which, I guess, is a stress dream, but it definitely wasn't about the lobby incident. So, yeah, no nightmares about the angry guest or anything. When I'm asleep, my brain seems to just do its own weird thing, it's really just when I'm awake that the images keep popping up like I was saying before.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any social activities or places outside of work since the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Um... outside of work? No, not really. I mean, my social life hasn't really changed much. I still go to my Thursday night trivia league at this pub down the street from my apartment—we actually came in second place last week, missed first by one point because we couldn't remember the capital of North Dakota, which is Bismarck, obviously, I should have known that. And I still go to the grocery store, the gym... well, I say the gym, I really only go like twice a week and mostly just walk on the treadmill while listening to podcasts. But I haven't stopped doing any of that stuff. \n\nIt's really just... like I was saying before, it's just the lobby at work. That's the only place I'm avoiding. I just keep taking that dingy back hallway with the flickering fluorescent lights that I mentioned. Because, like I said, if I even try to walk past that exact spot by the front counter where the guy was standing... where everything just went flying... my chest just gets so incredibly tight. It's like this heavy pressure, and I just get this overwhelming urge to bolt, to just get away from that specific area. So outside of work, I'm totally fine, going about my normal business, running errands, seeing friends. But inside the building? Yeah, I'm just sticking to the maintenance hallway to avoid that spot in the lobby. It's just easier that way, you know? Why put myself through that tight-chest feeling if I can just take a detour past the loading dock?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself startling more easily in general, not just in response to noises that remind you of the incident?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, honestly, that's... that's actually the main reason I decided I needed to come in and talk to someone. Because it's not just the lobby, it's... it's kind of everywhere now, and it's exhausting. Like, if a door slams—even if it's just the wind catching the heavy glass doors at the entrance, or the maintenance guy letting the utility closet door slip from his hand—I just practically jump out of my skin. And suitcases, oh man, the suitcases. We get a lot of people coming straight from the airport with those hard-shell roller bags, right? And the tile in our building has these wide grout lines, so when they roll them fast, it makes this loud, clattering *thwack-thwack-thwack* sound. \n\nBefore the incident, I wouldn't have even blinked at it. It was just background noise, just part of the ambient sound of the building. But now? Every single time someone comes rushing in with one of those bags, or if a door shuts a little too hard, or even if someone just drops a thick stack of mail on a desk nearby... I flinch so hard my shoulders end up by my ears. It's like my brain instantly thinks, *Oh god, it's happening again, he's throwing things again.* And then my heart just starts hammering against my ribs, just racing like I ran a sprint, and I have to sit there for like a full minute, taking these deep breaths, just trying to get my heart rate back down to normal. It's incredibly embarrassing if someone sees me do it, too. I just look so jumpy and on edge all the time, over completely normal, everyday sounds. It's just... I can't seem to turn off that alarm bell in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on your work or daily tasks since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It’s been so frustrating, to be honest with you. Because normally, I’m pretty sharp at the desk. I mean, I’ve been doing this for a while, so I know our reservation system inside and out—it's this older, kind of clunky software where you have to use a lot of F-keys instead of just clicking a mouse, but I used to just fly right through it. At the desk I used to move through reservations pretty smoothly, you know? I could check a guest in, swipe their card, and assign a room while basically having a full conversation with them about their flight or the weather. \n\nBut lately... man, lately it’s like my brain is just wading through molasses. I’ll be looking right at a guest's profile, and I just keep rereading the exact same screen over and over again. The words are right there, like their name and their check-out date, but the information just doesn't register in my head. And I find myself second-guessing the most incredibly simple choices. Like room notes, for example. Normally, if someone needs a feather-free room or they requested extra towels, I just type the code, hit F4, and I'm done. But now, I’ll just sit there staring at the keyboard, second-guessing simple choices like which room note to enter first. I'll actually debate with myself if I should put the late check-in note before the crib request, which literally does not matter at all, the housekeeping staff sees it all the same. \n\nBut I'll just freeze up, staring at the blinking cursor, wondering if I already typed it or if I'm putting it in the wrong field. And it's just a simple room note! It shouldn't take me three minutes to process something I've done a thousand times. But I just can't seem to hold my focus, and then I realize the guest is just standing there staring at me while I look at the screen, and I have to apologize and pretend the computer is being slow. It's just... I don't know, it's like my working memory is just completely shot right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do outside of work due to the way you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I was saying earlier about the trivia league, I'm still going to that. And I actually really enjoy it still. It's not like I'm sitting there at the pub dreading being there or anything. I still get really into it, especially the history rounds, because I watch a lot of those documentaries on streaming services—you know, the ones with the dramatic reenactments? I can binge those for hours on a Sunday afternoon. So when a history question comes up, I'm usually the one at the table who knows it, unless it's something really obscure about, like, 14th-century agricultural practices or something, but usually it's more mainstream history. \n\nAnd I'm still doing my regular stuff, like I said, the grocery store, the gym... I mean, I actually went to this outdoor farmer's market thing last weekend with a couple of friends. It was over in that park by the river, the one they just renovated with the new walking paths? They had all these local vendors selling artisanal cheeses and homemade jams and stuff. I ended up buying this really incredible spicy peach salsa that I've been putting on basically everything since Sunday. So, yeah, I was walking around there for like two hours, just enjoying the nice weather and chatting with my friends, and I didn't feel weird or disconnected or anything. \n\nIt really is just... it's just work. That's the only place where I feel this way. The second I walk out those sliding glass doors at the end of my shift, it's like this massive weight just lifts right off my shoulders, and I can breathe again. I can go home, I can watch my shows, I can go out for a burger with my buddy Dave—he always wants to go to this place that has these ridiculously huge onion rings, which I love, but they always give me terrible heartburn, but I eat them anyway. So, yeah, outside of that building, I'm still me. I'm still doing all my normal stuff and actually having fun doing it. It's just... it's just when I'm on the clock that everything feels so messed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your reactions to noises and memories of the incident are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... outside of work, no, like I was saying with the trivia and the farmer's market, my daily life is perfectly fine. But at work? Yeah. Yeah, it's definitely becoming a problem. I mean, I’m still showing up, right? I haven't called out sick or anything, because I really pride myself on being reliable. In the five years I've worked there, I've maybe called in sick twice, and one of those was when I had that awful stomach flu that was going around last winter. So I am still clocking in for my scheduled days. \n\nBut my manager—he's usually pretty observant about this stuff, he's been managing hotels for like twenty years—he actually had to shorten a couple of my shifts this past week. I'm actually really looking forward to it. I'm supposed to bring a potato salad. Outside of that, I'm just regular old me, hanging out and chatting with everyone just exactly like I always do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having persistent negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't... I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or that it was my fault or anything like that. I know I'm just the front desk clerk, you know? I was just doing my job. And as for other people... I mean, I don't think everyone is suddenly out to get me or that the world is this horribly evil place now. I know some people get really cynical after something crazy happens to them. Like, my uncle Gary, he got scammed by this shady roofing contractor a few years ago—the guy took his deposit and just completely vanished, disconnected his phone and everything—and ever since then, Gary thinks literally everyone is a criminal trying to pick his pocket. He won't even let the neighborhood kids shovel his driveway in the winter because he thinks it's some kind of racket. \n\nBut I'm not like that. I still think people are generally pretty decent. Sure, you get the occasional grumpy guest who yells at you because the ice machine on the fourth floor is broken—which, honestly, it's been broken for like three months and maintenance keeps saying they're waiting on a part from overseas, which I'm starting to think is just an excuse because how long does it take to ship a plastic valve, right? But even when guests get snappy about stuff like that, I don't think they're fundamentally bad people. They're just tired from traveling, or their flight got delayed, or whatever. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any dark, looming thoughts about humanity or myself or anything. I don't hate myself, I don't hate the guests, I don't think I'm broken. I'm just a normal guy trying to do my shifts and pay the rent on my apartment—which my landlord just raised by fifty bucks a month, by the way, which is super annoying, but what can you do? But yeah, no, my thoughts about myself and other people are pretty much exactly the same as they've always been. It's like this heavy pressure, and I just get this overwhelming urge to bolt, to just get away from that specific area. So outside of work, I'm totally fine, going about my normal business, running errands, seeing friends. But inside the building? Yeah, I'm just sticking to the maintenance hallway to avoid that spot in the lobby. It's just easier that way, you know? Why put myself through that tight-chest feeling if I can just take a detour past the loading dock?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself startling more easily in general, not just in response to noises that remind you of the incident?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, honestly, that's... that's actually the main reason I decided I needed to come in and talk to someone. Because it's not just the lobby, it's... it's kind of everywhere now, and it's exhausting. Like, if a door slams—even if it's just the wind catching the heavy glass doors at the entrance, or the maintenance guy letting the utility closet door slip from his hand—I just practically jump out of my skin. And suitcases, oh man, the suitcases. We get a lot of people coming straight from the airport with those hard-shell roller bags, right? And the tile in our building has these wide grout lines, so when they roll them fast, it makes this loud, clattering *thwack-thwack-thwack* sound. \n\nBefore the incident, I wouldn't have even blinked at it. It was just background noise, just part of the ambient sound of the building. But now? Every single time someone comes rushing in with one of those bags, or if a door shuts a little too hard, or even if someone just drops a thick stack of mail on a desk nearby... I flinch so hard my shoulders end up by my ears. It's like my brain instantly thinks, *Oh god, it's happening again, he's throwing things again.* And then my heart just starts hammering against my ribs, just racing like I ran a sprint, and I have to sit there for like a full minute, taking these deep breaths, just trying to get my heart rate back down to normal. It's incredibly embarrassing if someone sees me do it, too. I just look so jumpy and on edge all the time, over completely normal, everyday sounds. It's just... I can't seem to turn off that alarm bell in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on your work or daily tasks since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I really have. It’s been so frustrating, to be honest with you. Because normally, I’m pretty sharp at the desk. I mean, I’ve been doing this for a while, so I know our reservation system inside and out—it's this older, kind of clunky software where you have to use a lot of F-keys instead of just clicking a mouse, but I used to just fly right through it. At the desk I used to move through reservations pretty smoothly, you know? I could check a guest in, swipe their card, and assign a room while basically having a full conversation with them about their flight or the weather. \n\nBut lately... man, lately it’s like my brain is just wading through molasses. I’ll be looking right at a guest's profile, and I just keep rereading the exact same screen over and over again. The words are right there, like their name and their check-out date, but the information just doesn't register in my head. And I find myself second-guessing the most incredibly simple choices. Like room notes, for example. Normally, if someone needs a feather-free room or they requested extra towels, I just type the code, hit F4, and I'm done. But now, I’ll just sit there staring at the keyboard, second-guessing simple choices like which room note to enter first. I'll actually debate with myself if I should put the late check-in note before the crib request, which literally does not matter at all, the housekeeping staff sees it all the same. \n\nBut I'll just freeze up, staring at the blinking cursor, wondering if I already typed it or if I'm putting it in the wrong field. And it's just a simple room note! It shouldn't take me three minutes to process something I've done a thousand times. But I just can't seem to hold my focus, and then I realize the guest is just standing there staring at me while I look at the screen, and I have to apologize and pretend the computer is being slow. It's just... I don't know, it's like my working memory is just completely shot right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do outside of work due to the way you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, like I was saying earlier about the trivia league, I'm still going to that. And I actually really enjoy it still. It's not like I'm sitting there at the pub dreading being there or anything. I still get really into it, especially the history rounds, because I watch a lot of those documentaries on streaming services—you know, the ones with the dramatic reenactments? I can binge those for hours on a Sunday afternoon. So when a history question comes up, I'm usually the one at the table who knows it, unless it's something really obscure about, like, 14th-century agricultural practices or something, but usually it's more mainstream history. \n\nAnd I'm still doing my regular stuff, like I said, the grocery store, the gym... I mean, I actually went to this outdoor farmer's market thing last weekend with a couple of friends. It was over in that park by the river, the one they just renovated with the new walking paths? They had all these local vendors selling artisanal cheeses and homemade jams and stuff. I ended up buying this really incredible spicy peach salsa that I've been putting on basically everything since Sunday. So, yeah, I was walking around there for like two hours, just enjoying the nice weather and chatting with my friends, and I didn't feel weird or disconnected or anything. \n\nIt really is just... it's just work. That's the only place where I feel this way. The second I walk out those sliding glass doors at the end of my shift, it's like this massive weight just lifts right off my shoulders, and I can breathe again. I can go home, I can watch my shows, I can go out for a burger with my buddy Dave—he always wants to go to this place that has these ridiculously huge onion rings, which I love, but they always give me terrible heartburn, but I eat them anyway. So, yeah, outside of that building, I'm still me. I'm still doing all my normal stuff and actually having fun doing it. It's just... it's just when I'm on the clock that everything feels so messed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your reactions to noises and memories of the incident are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... outside of work, no, like I was saying with the trivia and the farmer's market, my daily life is perfectly fine. But at work? Yeah. Yeah, it's definitely becoming a problem. I mean, I’m still showing up, right? I haven't called out sick or anything, because I really pride myself on being reliable. In the five years I've worked there, I've maybe called in sick twice, and one of those was when I had that awful stomach flu that was going around last winter. So I am still clocking in for my scheduled days. \n\nBut my manager—he's usually pretty observant about this stuff, he's been managing hotels for like twenty years—he actually had to shorten a couple of my shifts this past week. He pulled me aside into the back office, the one with the really squeaky chair that we keep meaning to replace, and said he noticed I was just... struggling. Because, like I mentioned with the computer system earlier, I’m just so much slower at the reservation desk right now. It's taking me forever to get through a line of check-ins, and he could see the queue backing up all the way to the revolving doors, which is a huge metric they track for guest satisfaction. \n\nAnd the other thing, and this is probably the main reason he sent me home early on Tuesday... I keep finding excuses not to cross the lobby. Which is a huge part of the job! Like, if a guest needs help with the self-serve checkout kiosk, or if someone leaves the local attraction brochure rack all messy over by the concierge stand, normally I'd just pop out from behind the desk, walk across the main floor—which is right where it all happened, right in the center of that big open space—and take care of it. But now? I'll literally pretend I have to restock the blank room keys, or I'll ask one of the bellhops to go do it, or I'll just suddenly decide I desperately need to reorganize the lost and found drawer in the back. I just... I can't bring myself to walk out into that open space where guests are milling around. I feel too exposed. So I just stay glued to my terminal, and my manager definitely noticed I wasn't circulating and helping people in the lobby like I'm supposed to. So, yeah, it's definitely interfering with my actual job duties right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with your relationships with friends or family because of how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not at all. My relationships are totally fine. Like I was saying about Dave, the guy who always wants those massive onion rings? We actually hung out just yesterday, too. He was telling me all about his new car—he just bought this slightly used sedan, a hybrid, because he's got this crazy long commute now since his company moved their main office way out into the suburbs... but anyway, we were talking at the diner for like two or three hours, and it was totally normal. I didn't feel snappy or irritable with him, and I was completely engaged in what he was saying. \n\nAnd my family is good, too. My mom calls me every Sunday evening, practically like clockwork. Usually right after she finishes watching her Sunday night British mystery shows, she'll call me up and we talk for a good forty-five minutes about everything and nothing. Like this past week, she was going on and on about her neighbor's dog digging up her petunias, and I was just sitting there on my couch, listening and laughing with her. I didn't feel disconnected from her at all. She didn't even notice anything was off with me, because, honestly, when I'm at home on the phone with her, nothing really is off. \n\nSo, yeah, nobody's mad at me, I'm not pulling away from anyone or getting into arguments. My sister even texted me this morning to see if I wanted to come over for a backyard barbecue this weekend because her kids are finally out of school for the summer, and I immediately said yes. I'm actually really looking forward to it. I'm supposed to bring a potato salad. I usually just buy the one from the deli down the street from my apartment because I'm absolutely terrible at boiling potatoes—they always end up either way too mushy or they're still crunchy in the middle, which is just the worst texture, you know? But yeah, no, my friends and family... everything is completely fine there. It really is just that building. Just the hotel. Outside of that, I'm just regular old me, hanging out and chatting with everyone just exactly like I always do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having persistent negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't... I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or that it was my fault or anything like that. I know I'm just the front desk clerk, you know? I was just doing my job. And as for other people... I mean, I don't think everyone is suddenly out to get me or that the world is this horribly evil place now. I know some people get really cynical after something crazy happens to them. Like, my uncle Gary, he got scammed by this shady roofing contractor a few years ago—the guy took his deposit and just completely vanished, disconnected his phone and everything—and ever since then, Gary thinks literally everyone is a criminal trying to pick his pocket. He won't even let the neighborhood kids shovel his driveway in the winter because he thinks it's some kind of racket. \n\nBut I'm not like that. I still think people are generally pretty decent. Sure, you get the occasional grumpy guest who yells at you because the ice machine on the fourth floor is broken—which, honestly, it's been broken for like three months and maintenance keeps saying they're waiting on a part from overseas, which I'm starting to think is just an excuse because how long does it take to ship a plastic valve, right? But even when guests get snappy about stuff like that, I don't think they're fundamentally bad people. They're just tired from traveling, or their flight got delayed, or whatever. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any dark, looming thoughts about humanity or myself or anything. I don't hate myself, I don't hate the guests, I don't think I'm broken. I'm just a normal guy trying to do my shifts and pay the rent on my apartment—which my landlord just raised by fifty bucks a month, by the way, which is super annoying, but what can you do? But yeah, no, my thoughts about myself and other people are pretty much exactly the same as they've always been. It's just my reaction to being out in that specific open lobby space that's the problem right now, not how I actually think about people.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places or situations outside of work that you've been avoiding since the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not outside of work. Like I said, the farmer's market, the diner, my apartment... everywhere else is completely fine. It really is just the hotel. But even inside the hotel, it's not like I'm avoiding the break room or the supply closet or anything. It's really just... that specific spot. The lobby. \n\nLike, for instance, when I come in for my shift, I usually park in the employee lot out back, right? And normally, I'd just walk through the main entrance, say hi to the bellmen, maybe grab a coffee from the little cafe stand in the corner of the lobby before I clock in. But lately... I just can't do it. I've actually started taking the back hallway. It's this long, dingy corridor that runs behind the kitchens and the laundry room—it always smells faintly of bleach and old cooking oil, and the fluorescent lights in there flicker constantly, which gives me a bit of a headache if I look at them too long. But I've been taking that route every single day since it happened. \n\nBecause if I try to walk through the main lobby, especially past that one spot right near the grand staircase where... you know, where the guy was screaming and throwing things... my chest just tightens up. It's like this immediate, physical reaction. My breathing gets shallow, my heart starts pounding, and I just get this overwhelming urge to turn around and get as far away from that spot as possible. It's ridiculous, I know, because it's just a floor with a rug on it, but I just can't bring myself to walk past it if I don't absolutely have to. So, yeah, the back hallway it is, even if it means dodging laundry carts and dealing with the flickering lights. It's just easier than feeling like I can't breathe out in the main lobby.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's been about two weeks now. Yeah, I'm pretty sure it's been exactly two weeks, actually, because I remember it was a Tuesday. Tuesdays are usually our absolute slowest day for check-ins, which is why management only schedules two of us at the front desk. Normally on a weekend, like a Friday or Saturday, we have four clerks, plus a concierge and sometimes a floater, but on a Tuesday afternoon it's just me and usually Sarah. And of course, Sarah was in the break room taking her lunch when the guy actually came down and started screaming and threatening me at the desk. 

So yeah, ever since that Tuesday... it's been about fourteen days. And it's funny how time works, right? Because on one hand, those two weeks have absolutely flown by. I mean, regular life just keeps happening—my rent was due, I had to take my cat to the vet for her annual shots, which is a whole ordeal in itself because she completely turns into a demon the second she sees the carrier, so I've been busy. But on the other hand, every time I have to clock in for a shift and I have to detour down that dingy back hallway just to avoid the lobby, it feels like this whole thing has been going on forever. 

I honestly thought it would just kind of fade away after a couple of days, you know? Like, you have a really terrible, stressful day at work, you go home, you sleep it off, maybe you vent to a friend over some coffee or whatever, and you're good to go by Thursday. You just brush it off. But it hasn't faded at all. It's just been this constant, annoying thing for two whole weeks now, ever since that guy threatened me. Every single shift for two weeks, I'm doing this ridiculous dance around the lobby.

Sure, you get the occasional grumpy guest who yells at you because the ice machine on the fourth floor is broken—which, honestly, it's been broken for like three months and maintenance keeps saying they're waiting on a part from overseas, which I'm starting to think is just an excuse because how long does it take to ship a plastic valve, right? But even when guests get snappy about stuff like that, I don't think they're fundamentally bad people. They're just tired from traveling, or their flight got delayed, or whatever. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any dark, looming thoughts about humanity or myself or anything. I don't hate myself, I don't hate the guests, I don't think I'm broken. But yeah, no, my thoughts about myself and other people are pretty much exactly the same as they've always been. It's just my reaction to being out in that specific open lobby space that's the problem right now, not how I actually think about people.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places or situations outside of work that you've been avoiding since the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not outside of work. The lobby. \n\nLike, for instance, when I come in for my shift, I usually park in the employee lot out back, right? And normally, I'd just walk through the main entrance, say hi to the bellmen, maybe grab a coffee from the little cafe stand in the corner of the lobby before I clock in. But lately... I just can't do it. I've actually started taking the back hallway. It's this long, dingy corridor that runs behind the kitchens and the laundry room—it always smells faintly of bleach and old cooking oil, and the fluorescent lights in there flicker constantly, which gives me a bit of a headache if I look at them too long. But I've been taking that route every single day since it happened. \n\nBecause if I try to walk through the main lobby, especially past that one spot right near the grand staircase where... you know, where the guy was screaming and throwing things... my chest just tightens up. It's like this immediate, physical reaction. My breathing gets shallow, my heart starts pounding, and I just get this overwhelming urge to turn around and get as far away from that spot as possible. It's ridiculous, I know, because it's just a floor with a rug on it, but I just can't bring myself to walk past it if I don't absolutely have to. So, yeah, the back hallway it is, even if it means dodging laundry carts and dealing with the flickering lights. It's just easier than feeling like I can't breathe out in the main lobby.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms since the incident with the angry guest?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about two weeks now. Normally on a weekend, like a Friday or Saturday, we have four clerks, plus a concierge and sometimes a floater, but on a Tuesday afternoon it's just me and usually Sarah. No, not really. I mean, I don't get sweaty or start shaking or anything like that.
